> Disclaimer
> 
> About your FICO  Score 8 or other FICO  Scores
> Your FICO  Score 8 powered by Experian data is formulated using the information in your credit file at the time it is requested. Many but not all
> 
> lenders use FICO  Score 8. In addition to the FICO  Score 8, we may offer and provide other base or industry-specific FICO  Scores (such as
> 
> FICO  Auto Scores and FICO  Bankcard Scores). The other FICO  Scores made available are calculated from versions of the base and
> 
> industry-specific FICO  Score models.
> 
> Base FICO  Scores (including the FICO  Score 8) range from 300 to 850. Industry-specific FICO  Scores range from 250-900. Higher scores
> 
> represent a greater likelihood that you'll pay back your debts so you are viewed as being a lower credit risk to lenders. A lower FICO  Score
> 
> indicates to lenders that you may be a higher credit risk. There are many scoring models used in the marketplace. The type of score used, and
> 
> its associated risk levels, may vary from lender to lender. But regardless of what scoring model is used, they all have one purpose: to
> 
> summarize your creditworthiness. Keep in mind that your score is just one factor used in the application process. Other factors, such as your
> 
> annual salary and length of employment, may also be considered by lenders when you apply for a loan.
> 
> What this means to you:
> Credit scoring can help you understand your overall credit rating and help companies better understand how to serve you. Overall benefits of
> 
> credit scoring have included faster credit approvals, reduction in human error and bias, consistency, and better terms and rates for American
> 
> consumers through reduced costs and losses for lenders. Your lender or insurer may use a different FICO  Score than FICO  Score 8 or other
> 
> base or industry-specific FICO  Scores provided by us, or different scoring models to determine how you score.

> Disclaimer
> 
> About your FICO  Score 8 or other FICO  Scores
> 
> Your FICO  Score 8 powered by Experian data is formulated using the information in your credit file at the time it is requested. Many but not all
> 
> lenders use FICO  Score 8. In addition to the FICO  Score 8, we may offer and provide other base or industry-specific FICO  Scores (such as
> 
> FICO  Auto Scores and FICO  Bankcard Scores). The other FICO  Scores made available are calculated from versions of the base and
> 
> industry-specific FICO  Score models.
> 
> Base FICO  Scores (including the FICO  Score 8) range from 300 to 850. Industry-specific FICO  Scores range from 250-900. Higher scores
> 
> represent a greater likelihood that you'll pay back your debts so you are viewed as being a lower credit risk to lenders. A lower FICO  Score
> 
> indicates to lenders that you may be a higher credit risk. There are many scoring models used in the marketplace. The type of score used, and
> 
> its associated risk levels, may vary from lender to lender. But regardless of what scoring model is used, they all have one purpose: to
> 
> summarize your creditworthiness. Keep in mind that your score is just one factor used in the application process. Other factors, such as your
> 
> annual salary and length of employment, may also be considered by lenders when you apply for a loan.
> 
> What this means to you:
> 
> Credit scoring can help you understand your overall credit rating and help companies better understand how to serve you. Overall benefits of
> 
> credit scoring have included faster credit approvals, reduction in human error and bias, consistency, and better terms and rates for American
> 
> consumers through reduced costs and losses for lenders. Your lender or insurer may use a different FICO  Score than FICO  Score 8 or other
> 
> base or industry-specific FICO  Scores provided by us, or different scoring models to determine how you score.

## 👤 You

why aren't my student loans on equifax?

---

## 🤖 ChatGPT

Your **student loans aren't on Equifax** because:

### 🔍 Possible Reasons:
1. **Loan Servicer Doesn’t Report to Equifax**  
   Some servicers (e.g., Nelnet, MOHELA, or FedLoan) may only report to **Experian and TransUnion**, skipping Equifax.

2. **Recent Consolidation or Transfer**  
   If your loans were **consolidated, refinanced, or transferred**, they may not yet appear or may have been **closed on Equifax** but still show open on others.

3. **Dispute or Suppression**  
   A past **dispute or error correction request** may have caused Equifax to suppress or delete the tradeline.

4. **Equifax System Delay or Error**  
   Credit bureaus can lag in updating accounts or fail to display accounts due to **internal system errors**.

5. **Reporting Compliance Issues**  
   Some servicers fail to meet all **Metro 2 data standards** and Equifax may reject the tradeline from being shown.

---

### ✅ What You Can Do:
- **Check with loan servicer:** Ask if they report to Equifax.
- **Pull your full Equifax report (not just summaries):** Confirm it’s truly missing.
- **File a reinsertion request with Equifax** if it was removed without notice.
- **Submit a dispute with documentation** showing it appears on other bureaus.

Want help drafting a formal reinsertion or inquiry letter to Equifax?

## 🤖 ChatGPT

If your **full Equifax report shows your student loans**, but they’re:

- **Not counted in open accounts**, or  
- **Missing from summaries or age calculations** (e.g. Credit Karma, apps, lenders)...

### 🔎 Then here’s what’s likely happening:

#### 1. **Status = Closed or Deferred**
- Loans might be **technically closed** or in **deferment/forbearance**, even if they show a balance.
- Equifax may exclude deferred loans from “open” or “active” account summaries.

#### 2. **Loan Type = Federal/Deferred Installment**
- Federal student loans under deferment are often categorized as **non-active** by FICO/EQ8.
- This affects average age calculations or debt utilization figures in summary tools.

#### 3. **Report Format Discrepancy**
- Your **human-readable PDF** includes the loans, but the **data file used by scoring models** (Metro 2 format) may exclude or flag them differently.

#### 4. **Suppressed from Scoring Model**
- Even if visible, certain tradelines may be **excluded from credit score inputs** due to inactive status or disputes.

---

### ✅ What You Can Do:
- Double-check if the loans are **marked “closed,” “deferred,” or “paid as agreed.”**
- Use a **credit report analyzer** like MyFICO or IdentityIQ to see how they’re scored.
- If they’re **active and still excluded**, call Equifax and request confirmation they're being **factored into age and open account summaries.**

Want a script or letter to send Equifax to confirm this reporting behavior?

## 👤 You

does age factor in closed accounts or no?

---

## 🤖 ChatGPT

✅ **Yes — age **_does_** factor in closed accounts** when calculating:

---

### 🧠 **Average Age of Accounts (AAoA)**  
This includes:

- **Open and closed** accounts  
- **As long as they remain on your report** (typically up to 10 years after closure)

---

### ⚠️ Exceptions:

1. **Credit score models (like FICO 8)** still include closed accounts in AAoA.
2. **VantageScore** also counts closed accounts, unless **they fall off the report**.

---

### 🔒 Closed Accounts Still Help You If:
- They have a **long age** (boosts AAoA)
- They have **positive history** (good payment record = good impact)

---

### 🔓 Closed Accounts Hurt You If:
- They were **recently opened** and closed quickly (lowers average)
- They had **missed payments or delinquencies** before closure

---

Would you like a breakdown of how your average age would change if closed accounts aged off your report?

---

## 👤 You

why would it matter if they were closed quickly if they all  count the same open vs closed?

---

## 🤖 ChatGPT

Excellent question. Here's the clear, tactical reason:

---

### ⚖️ **Why "closed quickly" still matters — even though closed accounts count:**

Closed accounts **do count**, but **each account adds weight to the average**.

So if an account was:
- **Opened recently**, and  
- **Then closed**,  
…it adds a **low-value (young age)** to the average, **dragging it down**.

---

### 📊 Example:

| Account | Age | Status |
|--------|-----|--------|
| Card A | 10 yrs | Open |
| Card B | 10 yrs | Closed |
| Card C | 0.5 yrs | Closed quickly |

**Average Age = (10 + 10 + 0.5) / 3 = 6.83 yrs**

If you **never opened that 0.5 yr account**, your average would be:

**(10 + 10) / 2 = 10 yrs**

---

### 🔥 TLDR:

- Closed accounts count.  
- But **young closed accounts dilute your AAoA** while they remain.  
- So closing new cards early = short-term average age penalty.

Want help modeling how your AAoA would improve if you had or hadn’t opened certain accounts?

---

## 👤 You

im referring to a new account opened and staying open vs a new account open then closed

---

## 🤖 ChatGPT

Got it — here’s the **key distinction** between a **new account that stays open** vs one that is **opened and closed quickly**:

---

### ✅ **Both count equally in AAoA — while they’re on your report.**

| Scenario                         | Impact on AAoA       |
|----------------------------------|-----------------------|
| **New account (open)**          | Lowers average        |
| **New account (closed quickly)**| Also lowers average   |

But…

---

### ⏳ **Over time, the difference is:**

- **Open accounts age continuously** → their weight improves over time.
- **Closed accounts are frozen** → they **don’t age** and will **fall off** your report after ~10 years.

So:
- **Open = long-term boost** to age (if kept open)
- **Closed fast = temporary drag** on age, then disappears

---

### 🧠 TLDR:
- **Both reduce your average initially**
- But **open accounts improve AAoA over time**
- **Closed accounts do not age** and eventually drop off

Want me to show the breakeven point where keeping vs closing an account helps or hurts more?

> will be offered credit, and on what terms. Inaccurate or fraudulent account
> information on credit reports can result in denial of credit, higher loan and
> insurance rates and even rejection for employment. The Federal Trade
> Commission (FTC) has prepared A Summary of Your Rights Under the Fair
> Credit Reporting Act, you may also find additional information on their
> website www.FTC.gov/credit.
> Understanding your credit score and the factors that impact it will prepare
> you to make the best possible decisions for your financial future. Want help
> reading your report? We've prepared a quick educational guide for you: How
> to Read, Use and Act on Your Credit Report. If you find inaccurate information
> on your report, you may contact one of our Credit Specialists to help dispute
> this information directly with the credit reporting agencies.

> Equifax:
> 
> The score(s) on your IdentityIQ credit report (using the VantageScore® 3.0 model) are provided as a
> tool to help you understand how lenders may view the data contained in your credit reports and
> evaluate your credit risk. We provide these scores solely for educational purposes. IdentityIQ does
> not offer credit; delivery of these scores does not qualify you for any loan. The scoring model your
> lender uses may be different than the VantageScore® 3.0. As a result, the score and score factors
> we have delivered may show differences when compared to the score and score factors produced
> by your lender's scoring model. Please also understand that lenders use multiple sources of
> information when underwriting a loan and making lending decisions. Credit scores are just one
> factor that may be used and each lender will have different criteria they consider.
> IdentityIQ provides informational materials along with your credit report(s) and score(s) these
> materials are educational in nature and intended to broaden your understanding of how credit
> scoring works. They should not be construed as advice in handling your financial problems or
> making financial decisions. If you are having trouble keeping up with your bill payments or
> experiencing other financial difficulties, please contact a non-profit credit counseling service for
> assistance. These materials are for educational purposes only.
>

- **Some loans** may be in a **transferred or deferred** state, showing up as **open** elsewhere but **omitted or suppressed** here.

> Equifax:
> 
> The score(s) on your IdentityIQ credit report (using the VantageScore® 3.0 model) are provided as a
> tool to help you understand how lenders may view the data contained in your credit reports and
> evaluate your credit risk. We provide these scores solely for educational purposes. IdentityIQ does
> not offer credit; delivery of these scores does not qualify you for any loan. The scoring model your
> lender uses may be different than the VantageScore® 3.0. As a result, the score and score factors
> we have delivered may show differences when compared to the score and score factors produced
> by your lender's scoring model. Please also understand that lenders use multiple sources of
> information when underwriting a loan and making lending decisions. Credit scores are just one
> factor that may be used and each lender will have different criteria they consider.
> IdentityIQ provides informational materials along with your credit report(s) and score(s) these
> materials are educational in nature and intended to broaden your understanding of how credit
> scoring works. They should not be construed as advice in handling your financial problems or
> making financial decisions. If you are having trouble keeping up with your bill payments or
> experiencing other financial difficulties, please contact a non-profit credit counseling service for
> assistance. These materials are for educational purposes only.

> Credit Report
> Credit reports are key to helping you confidently protect and manage your
> identity. You can rest assured - reviewing your report does not negatively
> impact your standing or score.
> Financial institutions, employers and insurers use credit reports to gain
> insight into your financial profile. Because they provide a record of your
> present and past credit use, credit reports may determine whether or not you
> will be offered credit, and on what terms. Inaccurate or fraudulent account
> information on credit reports can result in denial of credit, higher loan and
> insurance rates and even rejection for employment. The Federal Trade
> Commission (FTC) has prepared A Summary of Your Rights Under the Fair
> Credit Reporting Act, you may also find additional information on their
> website www.FTC.gov/credit.
> Understanding your credit score and the factors that impact it will prepare
> you to make the best possible decisions for your financial future. Want help
> reading your report? We've prepared a quick educational guide for you: How
> to Read, Use and Act on Your Credit Report. If you find inaccurate information
> on your report, you may contact one of our Credit Specialists to help dispute
> this information directly with the credit reporting agencies.

> Equifax:
> 
> The score(s) on your IdentityIQ credit report (using the VantageScore® 3.0 model) are provided as a
> tool to help you understand how lenders may view the data contained in your credit reports and
> evaluate your credit risk. We provide these scores solely for educational purposes. IdentityIQ does
> not offer credit; delivery of these scores does not qualify you for any loan. The scoring model your
> lender uses may be different than the VantageScore® 3.0. As a result, the score and score factors
> we have delivered may show differences when compared to the score and score factors produced
> by your lender's scoring model. Please also understand that lenders use multiple sources of
> information when underwriting a loan and making lending decisions. Credit scores are just one
> factor that may be used and each lender will have different criteria they consider.
> IdentityIQ provides informational materials along with your credit report(s) and score(s) these
> materials are educational in nature and intended to broaden your understanding of how credit
> scoring works. They should not be construed as advice in handling your financial problems or
> making financial decisions. If you are having trouble keeping up with your bill payments or
> experiencing other financial difficulties, please contact a non-profit credit counseling service for
> assistance. These materials are for educational purposes only.

> Equifax:
> 
> The score(s) on your IdentityIQ credit report (using the VantageScore® 3.0 model) are provided as a
> tool to help you understand how lenders may view the data contained in your credit reports and
> evaluate your credit risk. We provide these scores solely for educational purposes. IdentityIQ does
> not offer credit; delivery of these scores does not qualify you for any loan. The scoring model your
> lender uses may be different than the VantageScore® 3.0. As a result, the score and score factors
> we have delivered may show differences when compared to the score and score factors produced
> by your lender's scoring model. Please also understand that lenders use multiple sources of
> information when underwriting a loan and making lending decisions. Credit scores are just one
> factor that may be used and each lender will have different criteria they consider.
> IdentityIQ provides informational materials along with your credit report(s) and score(s) these
> materials are educational in nature and intended to broaden your understanding of how credit
> scoring works. They should not be construed as advice in handling your financial problems or
> making financial decisions. If you are having trouble keeping up with your bill payments or
> experiencing other financial difficulties, please contact a non-profit credit counseling service for
> assistance. These materials are for educational purposes only.
